7 ML Frameworks

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

Why does the framework silently constrain every decision that comes after it?

Model equations do not execute themselves. A framework translates an architecture into work that hardware can run, and that translation is not neutral. It decides when operations execute, what intermediate state training retains, how memory is allocated, and which deployment targets can use the resulting artifact. These choices shape more than speed. They also determine what can be debugged directly, what the compiler can optimize, and whether the same model survives the path from a research environment to a production runtime. The same model expressed through two execution paths can differ in speed or memory use by several times, a gap that no amount of model tuning recovers when the framework hides the structure a compiler needs. The abstractions that make experimentation convenient therefore create long-lived commitments. Once a project accumulates checkpoints, modules, data loaders, serving formats, and team expertise around a framework, changing it becomes a system migration rather than a library substitution, measured in engineer-months rather than afternoons. A framework well suited to rapid exploration can expose too little structure for efficient deployment, while a constrained execution path can sacrifice the flexibility needed during discovery. Because these choices meet in one software layer, a local convenience can become a downstream performance or portability constraint. The engineering question is not which framework is universally best, but which trade-offs the workload, team, and target machine can afford to make fixed. Understanding frameworks is therefore about reasoning through those system choices rather than learning transient API syntax, which changes with each release while the underlying trade-offs persist. In D·A·M terms, the framework mediates algorithm-machine co-design by turning algorithmic intent and data representation into executable work under machine constraints.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain how frameworks mediate algorithm-machine co-design through execution, differentiation, and hardware abstraction
  • Compare eager, graph, and just-in-time compiled execution using dispatch overhead, kernel fusion, compilation cost, and deployment constraints
  • Analyze how reverse-mode differentiation computes every gradient in one backward pass and which forward state it must save or recompute
  • Estimate host-to-device transfer time and explain how tensor layout, data type, and device placement determine which kernel runs and whether transfers overlap computation
  • Implement module abstraction patterns for parameter discovery, mode behavior, serialization, and hooks
  • Calculate training-step FLOPs, memory traffic, and dispatch overhead to identify whether a step is bound by dispatch, memory traffic, or arithmetic
  • Select a development framework and deployment path based on model, hardware, team, and deployment requirements
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

7.1 Three Framework Problems

A few lines of Python can express one decoding step of a language model: embed the newest token, pass it through every transformer block, and project the result onto the vocabulary. The accelerator never sees those lines. It sees a stream of kernels,1 the compiled routines it executes one launch at a time, together with memory allocations and copies, and the machine learning framework is the software that turns the first into the second. Network Architectures fixed what work each architecture performs. It left open when each operation runs, how its gradients are computed, and which kernel on which device executes it.

1 Kernel (GPU): In GPU programming, a kernel is the function dispatched to execute in parallel across thousands of threads. Each kernel launch costs about 5 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s of CPU-side overhead for parameter assembly and GPU signaling, and an eager framework’s Python dispatch adds about 10 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s more before the launch, the split this chapter’s overhead model uses. Small, unfused operations therefore spend more time on launch overhead \((\Latency)\) than on useful arithmetic. Reducing kernel count through fusion is a direct attack on the overhead term of the iron law.

GPT-2 XL, the book’s bandwidth-bound lighthouse, shows what those open questions cost. One batch-one decoding step reads 3 GB of FP16 weights, which takes about 0.9 ms at the 3.35 TB/s memory bandwidth of an H100, the book’s reference accelerator. Executed one operation at a time, the same step issues about 12 kernels in each of its 48 transformer blocks, 576 launches in all. At 5 μs of driver overhead per launch, launching alone takes 2.9 ms, about 3× the weight read. An eager Python framework adds 10 μs of dispatch to every operation, which raises the host’s time to issue the step to 8.6 ms, about 10× the time the accelerator needs to read its weights. The accelerator spends most of the step waiting for its next instruction. Whether the step costs one millisecond or ten depends on what the framework does with those few lines of Python: run each operation as it is called, or capture the sequence, fuse it into fewer kernels, and launch it as a unit.

Every ML framework, regardless of API or design philosophy, must solve three core problems. The first is the execution problem: deciding when and how computation runs. A framework can execute operations immediately as written (eager execution2) or first construct a computational graph3 (a record of operations and their data dependencies, developed in section 7.3.1) to optimize before launching device kernels (graph execution). The decoding step above is an execution problem, and this choice sets the trade-off between interactive debugging and whole-program compiler optimization.

2 Eager execution: This mode executes each operation immediately, which enables direct debugging with standard tools but sacrifices the global view needed for graph-level optimizations. Without capturing a larger region of computation, an eager runtime cannot fuse across that region or preplan its memory, leaving workload-dependent optimization opportunities for compilers such as torch.compile.

3 Computational graph: Optimizing before executing is the design choice that separates graph execution from eager execution. Capturing the program as a data structure lets a framework fuse compatible operations into fewer GPU kernels before execution, reducing launch overhead and intermediate memory traffic. The engineering cost of this visibility is that the executed program differs from the source code, making debugging harder, a trade-off every graph-based framework must justify against the performance gain.

With an execution strategy in place, the framework must solve the differentiation problem: computing gradients automatically. Because gradient-based training requires derivatives of a scalar loss with respect to millions or billions of parameters, manual calculus is impossible at scale. Frameworks implement automatic differentiation by registering derivative rules across supported operator compositions, chaining vector-Jacobian products while managing numerical precision and the memory overhead of intermediate activations retained for backward passes.

↩︎ Reverse-mode automatic differentiation and computational graph traversal for gradient computation are established in Gradient computation and backpropagation.

Tillet, Philippe, H. T. Kung, and David Cox. 2019. “Triton: An Intermediate Language and Compiler for Tiled Neural Network Computations.” Proceedings of the 3rd ACM SIGPLAN International Workshop on Machine Learning and Programming Languages, 10–19. https://doi.org/10.1145/3315508.3329973.
Nickolls, John, Ian Buck, Michael Garland, and Kevin Skadron. 2008. “Scalable Parallel Programming with CUDA: Is CUDA the Parallel Programming Model That Application Developers Have Been Waiting For?” Queue 6 (2): 40–53. https://doi.org/10.1145/1365490.1365500.

The third problem is hardware abstraction: targeting diverse hardware from a single interface. The same model definition should be expressible across CPUs, GPUs, Tensor Processing Units (TPUs), and mobile accelerators, even though each target presents distinct memory hierarchies, instruction sets, and interconnect bandwidths. For every operation the framework must choose which kernel runs on which device, with which memory layout and precision. Within the GPU domain, framework ecosystems increasingly expose custom-kernel languages such as Triton (Tillet et al. 2019), allowing developers to write high-performance, block-level kernels without hand-crafting low-level CUDA (NVIDIA’s GPU programming platform (Nickolls et al. 2008)) or device assembly.

These three problems are tightly coupled: the execution model dictates when differentiation data structures are recorded, while the hardware abstraction layer determines which graph transformations can be lowered to the target. In the terms of the iron law of ML systems (principle 3), the model architecture fixes the operation count \(\Operations\), and the framework shapes the remaining terms. Execution strategy bears most directly on the fixed overhead \(\Latency\), differentiation on the bytes moved \(\DataVolume\) (and on \(\Operations\), since the backward pass adds work), and abstraction on the hardware efficiency \(\EfficiencyHw\). These are emphases rather than exclusive assignments, since fusion, for example, cuts both launches and traffic, and the chapter uses this one mapping throughout. The framework plays the role for a model that a compiler plays for a C program. The correspondence is literal: modern frameworks include optimizing compilers, such as Accelerated Linear Algebra (XLA) (Google 2025) and TorchInductor (Ansel et al. 2024), that lower a captured graph to machine code for the target accelerator. The formal definition below makes that role precise and separates frameworks from numerical libraries such as NumPy.

Definition 7.1: Machine learning frameworks

Machine learning frameworks are software systems that translate high-level mathematical model definitions into hardware-optimized execution plans by managing the computational graph, automatic differentiation, kernel dispatch, and memory allocation across the hardware hierarchy.

  1. Significance: Frameworks shape every iron law term that the model itself does not fix: the bytes moved \((\DataVolume)\), the hardware efficiency achieved \((\EfficiencyHw)\), and the fixed overhead paid \((\Latency)\). Compiler-backed kernel fusion, for example, can eliminate writes and subsequent reads of intermediate values between compatible operations. Fusing a matrix multiplication, bias add, and rectified linear unit (ReLU) changes how the same mathematics reaches hardware without changing the model.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a numerical library such as NumPy, which normally evaluates each operation as it is called, an ML framework can capture or defer execution to analyze a larger computational graph and apply cross-operation optimizations: kernel fusion, memory layout transformations, and parallel scheduling. Those optimizations require visibility beyond separately dispatched operations.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that frameworks are interchangeable API wrappers. Framework choice determines which compiler paths are available. PyTorch can recover graph-level optimization from eager code through torch.compile(), while TensorFlow and JAX commonly rely on XLA-backed compilation paths that lower operations to target hardware. Moving from eager execution to a compiler path can change throughput materially, but the gain depends on model structure, shapes, operator support, and hardware.

An ML framework translates logical intent into physical execution under the constraints of the iron law. It decides how to partition computation across memory hierarchies, when to trade numerical precision for throughput, and how to schedule operations so that whichever term dominates (data movement \(\DataVolume\), arithmetic computation, or launch latency \(\Latency\)) is minimized. The framework is where the governing physics developed throughout this book becomes executable code.

This chapter follows a model’s graph downward, one level at a time (table 1): the decoding step above carries the execution level, and a two-layer MNIST training step, traced at the end, gathers every level. After a brief look at how the layers of the stack came to exist, the execution section takes the graph to kernels and decides when each operation runs. The differentiation section builds the backward graph that training adds and accounts for the values it must keep. The abstraction section reaches the bottom of the descent, where tensors occupy device memory and kernels operate on them. Modules then assemble those pieces back into a model, and the anatomy of one training step traces a single step through every level. The chapter closes with the major frameworks and how to choose among them.

Automating this machinery (recording operation graphs, allocating gradient buffers, traversing dependencies in reverse, and dispatching hardware kernels) did not happen all at once. Execution, differentiation, and abstraction emerged sequentially as responses to scaling bottlenecks in earlier numerical tools, a four-decade progression from hand-tuned assembly routines to compiled graph runtimes.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team reports that their model executes correctly on CPU but produces mismatched tensor shapes and silent numerical corruption when switched to a GPU backend because some operators silently default to a different memory layout (such as NCHW versus NHWC). Which of the three fundamental framework problems does this failure most directly expose?

    1. The hardware abstraction problem, because one unified model interface must preserve consistent semantic behavior, memory layouts, and numerical behavior across diverse hardware backends
    2. The execution problem, because the operators were evaluated eagerly instead of being captured into a static graph
    3. The differentiation problem, because the backward pass failed to propagate gradients through non-contiguous strides
    4. A data engineering pipeline defect unrelated to framework runtime responsibilities

  2. Explain how viewing an ML framework as a compiler that lowers a model graph to hardware, rather than merely a numerical library like NumPy, changes an engineer’s expectations regarding framework selection and optimization under the systems iron law.

  3. True or False: Two frameworks that expose nearly identical user-facing Python tensor APIs and target the same GPU hardware will necessarily provide equivalent graph-level operator fusion and ahead-of-time compilation capabilities.

  4. An engineering organization chose a specialized research framework for rapid prototyping, only to discover later that the framework lacks export paths to their production edge accelerators, requiring months of manual re-implementation. Applying the chapter’s infrastructure-commitment principle, what is the key systems insight?

    1. Framework choice is easily reversible because weight arrays can be loaded into any runtime with zero engineering overhead
    2. Framework selection functions as a durable infrastructure commitment whose reversal cost compounds across model checkpoints, serving runtimes, CI/CD pipelines, and hardware access
    3. The primary failure was selecting an overly compact model architecture that failed to saturate edge accelerator memory
    4. Hardware abstraction layers eliminate all differences between training frameworks and production inference engines

See Answers →

7.2 Evolution of Framework Abstractions

In 1979, writing a matrix multiplication in Fortran that used the hardware efficiently required deep knowledge of cache lines, register scheduling, and vector units. By the late 2010s, a single line of Python (torch.matmul(A, W)) could dispatch to a tuned implementation without the programmer knowing the details of the hardware. Four decades of abstraction closed that gap, each layer removing a bottleneck that made the previous generation impractical at scale. Each layer automated a job the layer below left to the programmer. History built the layers from the bottom up, starting with tuned kernels and ending with graph compilers; the rest of this chapter walks them from the top down, starting from the graph a model defines.

The first layer established reusable hardware primitives. The Basic Linear Algebra Subprograms (BLAS)4 standardized low-level linear-algebra operations (Lawson et al. 1979), and LAPACK (Anderson et al. 1999) built factorizations and eigensolvers on top of them. Naive nested loops saturate memory bandwidth long before the arithmetic units reach peak throughput. BLAS let numerical software delegate these operations to vendor libraries tuned for each processor’s cache sizes, vector widths, and register files. Its Level 3 extension added the matrix-matrix routines, such as matrix multiplication (C = A @ B),5 that dominate ML workloads (Dongarra et al. 1990). The second layer, NumPy6 (Harris et al. 2020), wrapped those libraries in Python: the interpreter manages control flow and array metadata (shape, strides, and data type), while compiled code runs the inner loops over contiguous buffers. That division of labor, high-level intent in an interpreted language and inner loops in native code, became the template for every framework that followed.

4 BLAS (basic linear algebra subprograms): The 1979 API specification at the bottom layer of the stack; it fixed a set of Fortran-callable vector operations and separated the public routine names from the machine-specific implementation beneath them. Every framework above it inherits the same division: call a standard linear-algebra routine from any language and let a tuned vendor library target the processor. On NVIDIA GPUs, the tuned path for general matrix multiply (GEMM), a Level 3 routine added in 1990, is cuBLAS (NVIDIA 2024).

NVIDIA. 2024. cuBLAS: CUDA Basic Linear Algebra Subprograms.
Lawson, Charles L., Richard J. Hanson, David R. Kincaid, and Fred T. Krogh. 1979. “Basic Linear Algebra Subprograms for Fortran Usage.” ACM Transactions on Mathematical Software 5 (3): 308–23. https://doi.org/10.1145/355841.355847.
Anderson, Edward, Zhaojun Bai, Christian Bischof, L. Susan Blackford, James Demmel, Jack Dongarra, Jeremy Du Croz, et al. 1999. LAPACK Users’ Guide. 3rd ed. SIAM. https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9780898719604.

5 GEMM: The matrix-matrix primitive behind C = A @ B. Hardware vendors hand-tune GEMM for their specific chips because dense layers, attention projections, and convolution lowering all rely on matrix multiplication, making this one routine a performance floor for many frameworks above it in the stack. Its high arithmetic intensity makes GEMM the operation most able to approach peak compute throughput, while small or misaligned shapes often fall back to much lower utilization.

Dongarra, Jack J., Jeremy Du Croz, Sven Hammarling, and Iain S. Duff. 1990. “A Set of Level 3 Basic Linear Algebra Subprograms.” ACM Transactions on Mathematical Software 16 (1): 1–17. https://doi.org/10.1145/77626.79170.

6 NumPy (numerical Python): In 2005, Travis Oliphant unified two competing Python array libraries (Numeric and Numarray) into a single BLAS-backed package. Its pattern of writing logic in Python and executing loops in compiled code became the design template for ML frameworks: PyTorch tensors and TensorFlow arrays extend the same \(\TensorAxisCount\)-dimensional array abstraction to GPUs.

Harris, Charles R., K. Jarrod Millman, Stéfan J. van der Walt, Ralf Gommers, Pauli Virtanen, David Cournapeau, Eric Wieser, et al. 2020. “Array Programming with NumPy.” Nature 585 (7825): 357–62. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-020-2649-2.

7 Theano: Developed at the Montreal Institute for Learning Algorithms (MILA) under Yoshua Bengio starting in 2007, Theano was an early and influential Python framework that compiled symbolic mathematical expressions into optimized CPU and GPU code via computational graphs (Bergstra et al. 2010; Team et al. 2016). It demonstrated that a Python-defined computation graph could be compiled for GPU execution without requiring the researcher to write CUDA code. Mila ended active development of Theano in 2017 (Mila 2026).

Bergstra, James, Olivier Breuleux, Frédéric Bastien, Pascal Lamblin, Razvan Pascanu, Guillaume Desjardins, Joseph Turian, David Warde-Farley, and Yoshua Bengio. 2010. “Theano: A CPU and GPU Math Compiler in Python.” Proceedings of the Python in Science Conference 4: 18–24. https://doi.org/10.25080/majora-92bf1922-003.
Team, The Theano Development, Rami Al-Rfou, Guillaume Alain, Amjad Almahairi, Christof Angermueller, Dzmitry Bahdanau, Nicolas Ballas, et al. 2016. “Theano: A Python Framework for Fast Computation of Mathematical Expressions.” arXiv Preprint.
Mila. 2026. Open Source Software.

The third layer automated gradient computation. NumPy users still derived and coded backpropagation by hand, an error-prone process that does not scale to deep networks. Deep learning frameworks such as Theano,7 TensorFlow (Abadi et al. 2016), and PyTorch (Paszke et al. 2019) made automatic differentiation a runtime primitive: by recording the forward computation as a graph, they construct the backward computation automatically. That automation let the bitter lesson operate at the pace of research. Once a new architecture no longer needed a hand-derived backward pass, general methods that scale with computation could be tried as quickly as their forward functions could be written.

↩︎ The bitter lesson, that general methods which exploit growing computation outlast hand-engineered approaches, is established in The Bitter Lesson.

Across that history (figure 1), the milestones run from BLAS and LAPACK routines through NumPy vectorization to symbolic graph capture in Theano and TensorFlow, dynamic graphs in PyTorch, and functional transformations in JAX. Each generation preserved the vendor-tuned kernel layer beneath it while raising the level of abstraction at which engineers express and differentiate models.

Each generation hid details that consumed engineering effort in the previous one, and each introduced new trade-offs. BLAS hid assembly-level optimization but fixed the interface. NumPy hid memory management but left differentiation manual. Modern frameworks hide gradient computation but force a choice among execution models. Every layer also hides assumptions about the data it passes along: each operation expects its inputs to have a particular shape, data type, device, and, for physical quantities, unit. When nothing checks those expectations at the boundary, correct components can compose into a wrong system.

War Story 7.1: The interface that forgot its units (1999)
Context: NASA’s Mars Climate Orbiter depended on ground software, spacecraft software, navigation teams, and contractor interfaces all agreeing on the physical meaning of exchanged values (Mars Climate Orbiter Mishap Investigation Board 1999).

Mechanism: Ground software supplied by Lockheed Martin computed thruster impulse in English units (pound-force seconds), while JPL navigation software consumed the values assuming SI units (newton-seconds), a 4.45\(\times\) implicit unit mismatch.

Impact: On September 23, 1999, the spacecraft was lost after passing behind Mars (occultation). Its corrected 57 km periapsis, rather than the planned 226 km, was judged too low for survival.

Response: The board recommended unit-consistency checks, specification audits of transferred data, and stronger end-to-end verification of ground software.

Systems lesson: Two pieces of correct code can compose into a wrong system when the meaning of the values they exchange is implicit. ML frameworks meet the same failure whenever tensor memory layouts (such as channels-first NCHW [\(\text{Batch} \times \text{Channels} \times \text{Height} \times \text{Width}\)] vs. channels-last NHWC [\(\text{Batch} \times \text{Height} \times \text{Width} \times \text{Channels}\)]), quantization scale factors, or physical feature units differ across pipeline stages. The outputs look plausible but quietly corrupt downstream inference. Frameworks guard against the tensor cases by checking, at every operation, that its inputs have the expected shape, data type, and device.

Mars Climate Orbiter Mishap Investigation Board. 1999. Mars Climate Orbiter Mishap Investigation Board Phase i Report. National Aeronautics; Space Administration.

A framework can run those checks as each operation executes, when the failing line and its intermediate values are directly visible, or before execution, when a captured graph can be validated and optimized as a whole. When checks and optimizations happen is the execution problem. It is the first framework design choice because runtime dispatch and graph capture determine both the debugging experience and what optimizations automatic differentiation and hardware mapping can later exploit.

Self-Check: Question

  1. While NumPy provided high-performance linear algebra by wrapping BLAS in Python, what critical scaling bottleneck did it leave unaddressed that motivated the development of deep learning frameworks such as Theano, TensorFlow, and PyTorch?

    1. Inability to execute matrix multiplications on single-core CPU architectures
    2. Lack of an \(n\)-dimensional array data structure in scientific computing
    3. The requirement for manual gradient derivation and hand-written backpropagation passes for multi-layer neural networks
    4. Inability to run compiled Fortran and C routines through high-level scripting languages

  2. Explain why the relationship between rungs on the ladder of abstraction (such as BLAS/LAPACK, NumPy, and modern deep learning frameworks) is characterized by inheritance rather than replacement.

  3. Order the following historical computing milestones in the evolution of numerical and machine learning software abstractions, from earliest (1979) to most recent (2018):

  4. JAX introduces functional composable transformations and XLA compilation

  5. BLAS standardizes reusable low-level linear algebra primitives

  6. Theano introduces compiled Python computational graphs for GPUs

  7. NumPy establishes Python’s unified \(n\)-dimensional array and vectorization standard

  8. PyTorch introduces dynamic define-by-run execution graphs

  9. LAPACK extends BLAS with higher-level numerical routines (e.g., SVD, factorizations)

  10. The architectural design pattern established by NumPy, where high-level control logic is written in an expressive interpreted language (such as Python) while inner numerical loops are delegated to compiled C/Fortran libraries, is known as ____.
