7.3 Execution: From Graphs to Kernels

Consider two engineers writing the same neural network. The first debugs interactively, printing tensor shapes after each operation, inspecting intermediate values, and stepping through code with pdb. The second waits through a compilation step, then watches a model built from many small operations run faster while losing that direct, line-by-line view of intermediate state. Both are correct; they have made different choices about the execution problem, the question of whether operations should execute immediately as written or be recorded for later execution. It sets debugging workflow, deployment options, and peak hardware utilization. The GPT-2 XL decoding step that opened this chapter is the case in point: issued one operation at a time, it keeps the accelerator waiting on the host for most of the step.

7.3.1 Computational graphs

If a runtime sees only one Python operation at a time and forgets it as soon as the kernel returns, it cannot know which intermediate tensors later gradient rules will need or which adjacent operations could share registers on the accelerator. To plan across operations, a framework represents the program the same way Network Architectures represented model architectures, as a computational graph: a directed acyclic graph (DAG) where nodes represent operations and edges represent data dependencies. The rest of this chapter follows that graph down toward the hardware. It is the same structure that backpropagation traverses in reverse, here serving as the framework’s internal model of the computation for scheduling and optimization as well.

A graph decouples the mathematical operations from the order in which hardware evaluates them. In the minimal case (figure 2), directed edges route input variables \(\FunctionInputVariable\) and \(\FunctionSecondInputVariable\) into the central transformation operator \(\IllustrativeFunction(\FunctionInputVariable, \FunctionSecondInputVariable)\), which emits the output variable \(\FunctionOutputVariable\).

Real machine learning models require much more complex graph structures. Figure 3 partitions this system interaction into two coupled domains: the left container isolates a six-node neural network DAG with explicit dataflow edges, while the right container houses the runtime components (memory management and device placement) that interface with it.

This graph representation is more than a visualization; it is the data structure that enables both efficient execution and automatic differentiation. Without a graph that spans multiple operations, a runtime must launch each operator in isolation and materialize every intermediate tensor in off-chip memory before the next kernel can read it.

7.3.2 The memory wall and kernel fusion

That isolation is costly whenever an unoptimized runtime evaluates a sequence of element-wise operations such as \(\text{LayerNorm} \to \text{Dropout} \to \text{ReLU}\) as three separate GPU kernels. The first reads inputs from off-chip high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and writes intermediate results back to HBM, the second reads those results and writes back again, and the third reads them a final time. Shuttling intermediate tensors across the external memory bus three times dwarfs the arithmetic time and leaves compute units idle.

This bottleneck stems from the memory wall, the widening gap between compute growth and memory-bandwidth growth that equation 2.3 quantifies. An accelerator performs arithmetic far faster than it fetches operands from off-chip memory. Most individual operation types in a neural network (activations, normalizations, and other element-wise operations) are therefore memory bound, limited by data movement. The large matrix multiplications that dominate total arithmetic can be compute bound, limited by arithmetic throughput. Performance Bounds formalizes the boundary between the two regimes. On-chip storage (registers and SRAM scratchpads) runs at tens of terabytes per second but holds only tens of megabytes, whereas off-chip HBM or GDDR holds tens to a few hundred GB behind external buses with hundreds of cycles of latency.

↩︎ The memory wall and the growth-rate ratio behind it are established in Physical Constraints on Deployment.

8 Fused attention kernel: A fused attention kernel combines the \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) product, softmax, and value-weighted output into a tiled implementation that keeps intermediate values in on-chip memory rather than materializing the full attention matrix in HBM. FlashAttention, introduced in Memory requirements, is the canonical named example. The framework lesson is broader than the specific algorithm: fusion can shift an operation’s position on the roofline model from bandwidth-limited toward throughput-limited execution by reducing round-trips through external memory.

Dao, T., D. Y. Fu, S. Ermon, A. Rudra, and C. Ré. 2022. “FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 16344–59. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1189.

Kernel fusion (also called operator fusion) fixes memory-bound chains by combining them into a single kernel that avoids intermediate HBM traffic. Instead of three round-trips to HBM, a fused kernel loads inputs from HBM once, evaluates normalization, dropout, and ReLU entirely within on-chip registers and SRAM, and writes only the final output tensor back to HBM, cutting memory traffic by roughly 3\(\times\). In general, for \(\TensorElementCount\) elements of \(\ElementStorageSize\) bytes each, fusing a chain of \(\ChainedOperationCount\) elementwise operations into one kernel reduces idealized read-and-write traffic from \(2\ChainedOperationCount \TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) to \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes, a derivation worked in Operator fusion. Fusion also removes all but one of the chain’s kernel launches. Attention kernels8 use the same principle at larger scale. Instead of materializing the full attention matrix in HBM, a fused implementation can keep tiles close to the compute units, cut HBM accesses by up to 9\(\times\), and produce workload-specific speedups from 15 percent to 3\(\times\) (Dao et al. 2022).

↪ The on-chip limits that decide which intermediates a fused kernel can keep on a specific accelerator are developed in Kernel fusion on the accelerator.

Framework compilers can fuse only operations that are visible to them simultaneously. When an eager runtime dispatches operations individually, each kernel launch executes in isolation, concealing cross-operation fusion and buffer-reuse opportunities. Capturing a broader region of computation into a deferred graph removes that isolation: the compiler can examine an entire sequence of dependencies, establish intermediate tensor lifetimes, and fuse compatible operations before dispatching work to the accelerator.

The computational graph enables global optimization, but the timing of its construction forces a four-way trade-off across debugging, optimization, deployment, and flexibility. Consider an elementary expression such as y = x * 2. Evaluating the multiplication immediately yields a concrete tensor in y so developers can inspect intermediate values and branch on computed tensor values with standard Python control flow, but the runtime sees only one operation at a time. Deferring execution records the operation symbolically into a dependency graph that can be fused, memory-planned, and exported to run without the host interpreter. Because no single execution model serves all four demands at once, modern frameworks span three strategies: eager execution with dynamic graphs, static computation graphs, and hybrid approaches that combine just-in-time (JIT) compilation with eager development.

7.3.3 Eager execution with dynamic graphs

When a tensor shape mismatches or an activation turns to NaN mid-layer, a deferred graph fails far from the Python line that created the operation, hiding the intermediate values that caused the fault. Eager execution, also called define-by-run, avoids that disconnect by evaluating each operation as soon as the program calls it and building the computation graph dynamically at runtime.

Example 7.1: Eager vs. graph execution code comparison
PyTorch (eager execution):

import torch

x = torch.tensor([1.0, 2.0])
y = x * 2
print(f"Intermediate value: {y}")  # Works immediately
z = y.sum()

TensorFlow 1.x (static graph):

import tensorflow as tf

x = tf.placeholder(tf.float32)
y = x * 2
# print(y) -> Prints Tensor("mul:0"...), not value!
z = tf.reduce_sum(y)

with tf.Session() as sess:
    result = sess.run(z, feed_dict={x: [1.0, 2.0]})

Systems insight: Eager execution exposes intermediate values as ordinary runtime state, which makes debugging direct. Static graphs stage computation before execution, which enables whole-graph optimization but changes the debugging model.

In this define-by-run model, when host code evaluates y = x * 2, the runtime dispatches the multiplication kernel to the device command queue and returns a tensor handle for subsequent operations. Device execution can continue asynchronously; dependent operations must wait for the result, and reading its value on the host can require synchronization.

Immediate evaluation gives eager code the flexibility of ordinary programming: developers can print intermediate values, use conditionals based on computed results, and debug with standard tools. The framework records operations as they happen, constructing a dynamic graph that reflects the actual execution path taken.

For gradient computation, the framework records a history of operations in what is called an autograd tape,9 a transient data structure built during execution. Each tensor operation creates a node that records: the operation performed, references to input tensors, and how to compute gradients. These nodes form a DAG that records the actual path taken during forward-pass execution rather than a graph fixed in advance. Listing 1 shows how PyTorch records operations as they execute in its default eager mode.

9 Autograd tape: The tape’s own nodes are small; its memory cost comes from the tensors they reference. That footprint grows with model depth and sequence length as saved activations accumulate until backpropagation releases them, which is why deep models retain only selected activations and recompute the rest (activation checkpointing) to avoid out-of-memory failures.

After these two operations, the framework has constructed an autograd tape with two nodes: one for the multiplication and one for the addition. The tape records that z depends on y, and y depends on x.

Calling z.backward() traverses this tape in reverse topological order, applying the chain rule at each node:

  1. Compute \(\frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVariable}{\partial \FunctionOutputVariable} = 1\) (seed gradient)
  2. Call AddBackward0.backward() \(\rightarrow \frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVariable}{\partial \TapeIntermediateResult} = 1\)
  3. Call MulBackward0.backward() \(\rightarrow \frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVariable}{\partial \FunctionInputVariable} = 2\)
  4. Accumulate gradient in x.grad

After backward() completes, autograd normally releases the saved state needed by its backward rules. Other live references can still retain tensor storage and graph nodes. The next forward pass builds a new graph. Values needed for gradients are saved during the forward pass and remain available until backward consumes them, so their memory cost spans both phases rather than appearing only during backward.

Example 7.2: In-place operations can break gradients
Scenario: An engineer applies an in-place mutation (x += 1) within a custom PyTorch activation function to reduce memory allocations.

Diagnosis: In-place operations overwrite tensor memory containing forward-pass activations required by the autograd tape for backward-pass gradient computation, triggering a runtime version-counter error (PyTorch Contributors 2026a). PyTorch enforces this safety invariant by maintaining an integer version counter on each tensor that increments on every in-place write. If a saved tensor’s version at backward execution differs from its version recorded during the forward pass, autograd throws an error, because the activation required by the derivative rule was modified in place.

Systems lesson: Framework automatic differentiation depends on immutable forward-pass activation records. In-place writes to a saved tensor invalidate the autograd record, so PyTorch stops with an error rather than compute a wrong gradient.

PyTorch Contributors. 2026a. PyTorch Autograd Mechanics.

Recording that tape and launching device work happen inside the same one-operation-at-a-time loop (figure 4). Python defines an operation, the runtime records its backward node and enqueues its device work, and control returns to Python for the next call while the device may execute asynchronously. When the runtime decides what to launch, it sees only the current operation rather than a whole region of future calls.

The dynamic autograd tape expresses data-dependent behavior directly in the host language. Conditionals and loops can depend on tensor values computed during execution, enabling algorithms such as beam search, dynamic-length recurrent models, or adaptive compute paths that adjust their execution based on intermediate activations. Static graphs can also represent data-dependent control flow and dynamic dimensions through graph operations, but eager execution makes these patterns natural to write and debug in Python. Because operations are issued immediately, developers can print tensors, inspect values, and use standard debuggers (pdb, breakpoints) to diagnose errors much as they would in another Python program.

This flexibility imposes latency costs that map directly to the iron law. Each training step constructs an autograd tape dynamically, adding host-side Python dispatch latency and tape allocation to \(\Latency\). Because every operation traverses the framework dispatcher before a device kernel launches, host overhead quickly dominates when individual kernels execute in single-digit microseconds. Eager execution also lacks a whole-graph view, preventing the framework from fusing element-wise chains into single GPU launches (which would cut \(\DataVolume\)) or pre-allocating a static memory arena for intermediate activations (which would cut allocation overhead in \(\Latency\)). The autograd tape must retain every tensor requested by registered backward rules, holding memory until backpropagation consumes it. Together, these costs impose a throughput ceiling on fine-grained workloads where dispatch latency overshadows device arithmetic.

7.3.4 Dispatch overhead in eager execution

That throughput ceiling appears whenever a kernel finishes in a few microseconds while the host-side interpreter and driver need tens of microseconds to issue it. The dispatch overhead ratio, host software time \(\HostSoftwareTime\) divided by device hardware time \(\DeviceHardwareTime\), quantifies this mismatch in an additive model without host-device overlap (asynchronous execution can hide part of the host cost when device queues stay full). When the ratio exceeds 1, host dispatch takes more than half of each step; the dispatch overhead law of section 7.3.7.2 later expresses the same ratio across a multi-operation workload through operation count and per-operation cost.

Fixed dispatch dominates small ops and fades behind large ones.

In the illustrative eager-execution model used here, every operation pays a fixed cost of approximately 15 μs for Python-side dispatch and driver launch combined, the same per-operation cost as the decoding step that opened the chapter. Actual overhead varies by framework, backend, and hardware, but its relative weight still depends on operation size. For a small operation such as a ReLU on a small vector, the kernel might execute in only 1 μs. Dispatch then takes 94 percent of the modeled step time, the dispatch overhead ratio sits far above 1, and the GPU spends most of its time waiting for the next command. For a large operation such as a large matrix multiply, the kernel executes for 100 μs, dispatch drops to 13 percent of the modeled step time, and the dispatch overhead ratio falls well below 1. The operation is device-work dominated; distinguishing compute from memory limits still requires comparing its work and traffic.

Dispatch overhead explains why models with many small layers run significantly slower than their raw FLOP count predicts. Acting on the binding term places this symptom in the bottleneck taxonomy, classifying a dispatch-dominated workload as overhead-bound rather than compute-bound and showing which optimizations actually move it. Removing that per-operation interpreter work requires moving from kernel-by-kernel dispatch to graph-level execution, either by defining a static computation graph ahead of time or by capturing and compiling regions just in time (section 7.3.6). A captured graph can still launch multiple kernels; fusion and runtime replay determine how much launch overhead remains.

↩︎ The MNIST epoch estimate, in which this fixed per-operation cost decides training time on a CPU and an accelerator alike, is established in Iterative learning process.

7.3.5 Static computation graphs

The overhead costs of eager execution motivate the opposite design: capturing the entire computation before executing any of it. Because an eager runtime sees only one operation at a time, it cannot fuse a chain of small kernels or plan buffer reuse across a whole layer before launching work on the accelerator. Static graph execution solves this visibility problem by defining the complete computational graph as a symbolic representation first and executing it in a separate phase. This “define-then-run” execution model means the graph exists before any computation occurs, enabling ahead-of-time analysis, transformation, and global optimization that separate eager calls cannot support unless a compiler captures them.

Static graphs implement a clear separation between graph construction and execution. TensorFlow 1.x, which exemplified this approach, makes the two phases explicit in code (listing 2). It deliberately runs the same x * 2 then + 1 computation shown under eager execution in listing 1, holding the arithmetic fixed so that the only thing that changes is when it executes. Symbolic definition creates placeholders and operations without computation, while explicit execution triggers actual arithmetic.

In the definition phase, the program declares operations and variables and builds the symbolic DAG without allocating any tensor memory. Only in the execution phase do data load, concrete buffers materialize on the accelerator, and the captured graph run (figure 5).

The key difference from eager execution is that during construction, x, y, and z are not tensors containing values but rather symbolic nodes in a graph. Operators such as * and + append transformation nodes to the graph definition without issuing arithmetic. Calling print(y) makes this distinction visible: it prints symbolic tensor metadata rather than numerical values. Only when the host invokes sess.run() does the runtime analyze, optimize, and execute the captured graph on device buffers.

Because the framework has the complete graph before execution, it can perform ahead-of-time optimization unavailable across uncaptured eager calls. The kernel fusion opportunity introduced in section 7.3.2 becomes actionable here. Because the framework sees y = x * 2 and z = y + 1 together in the graph, it can fuse them into z = x * 2 + 1, eliminating the intermediate y and halving idealized memory traffic. When tensor shapes and lifetimes are known, the compiler can plan memory before execution and reuse buffers whose lifetimes do not overlap; dynamic dimensions may require bounds or runtime allocation. Tensor layouts can also be transformed globally (for example, NCHW to NHWC) to match hardware preferences, though physical layout conversions may still require copies. Dead-code elimination (DCE)10 removes operations whose results are unused, and constant folding precomputes operations on constant values when their inputs are known. These optimizations map directly to iron law terms: kernel fusion can reduce \(\DataVolume\) by avoiding intermediate memory writes, constant folding reduces repeated work, planned allocation can reduce runtime overhead, and dead code elimination removes work and associated data movement when unused computations are present. Compilers such as XLA11 (Google 2025) extend this approach, compiling captured subgraphs into fused machine code specialized for target hardware.

10 Dead code elimination (DCE): Removes graph nodes whose results do not affect observable outputs or side effects. In ML graphs, dead code arises from unused branches or post-transform artifacts; side-effect analysis ensures safe removal to prevent execution of dead operations and reduce kernel launch overhead.

11 XLA (accelerated linear algebra): XLA fuses subgraphs, specializes layouts, and lowers high-level operations into backend-specific code, so its fusion attacks both launch overhead \((\Latency)\) and intermediate memory writes \((\DataVolume)\). Its speedup is therefore not a fixed multiplier. Large GEMM-heavy regions may already be compute bound, while chains of small elementwise operations benefit more because fusion removes repeated trips through external memory, so gains depend on graph structure, backend support, and input-shape stability.

Static graphs can therefore approach high utilization when shapes, operators, and layouts match efficient backend paths. The cost of this performance is reduced flexibility. Standard Python control flow (if, for) cannot depend on computed tensor values in static graphs. TensorFlow provides graph-level control flow primitives (tf.cond and tf.while_loop) that support data-dependent conditions, but these require special syntax that diverges from standard Python, making code harder to write and reason about. Debugging is difficult because stack traces point to graph construction code, not execution code. Error messages often reference symbolic node names rather than the actual operations that failed.

7.3.6 Graph capture and JIT compilation

Staying in eager mode leaves small-operation workloads bound by host dispatch and unfused HBM traffic, while rewriting a research model into a static graph gives up interactive Python debugging and standard control flow. JIT compilation bridges that gap by letting developers write and debug ordinary eager code while capturing regions of the program at runtime for graph-level optimization. The core trade-off in graph capture is fidelity vs. generality. Tracing captures the execution path taken during a sample run, producing high fidelity to that path but missing branches not taken. Source-level compilation (scripting) analyzes supported program structure, preserving supported control flow but requiring a restricted language subset. Both approaches produce an intermediate representation (IR)12 that enables graph optimizations such as kernel fusion, constant folding, dead code elimination, and buffer reuse.

12 Intermediate representation (IR): The “intermediate” captures this format’s architectural role: a language-independent layer that decouples the frontend (Python capture) from the backend (hardware code generation), exactly as LLVM IR decouples C/Rust/Swift frontends from x86/ARM backends. ML frameworks adopted this compiler pattern because it reduces the \(\mathcal{O}(\CompilerFrontendCount\times \CompilerBackendCount)\) cost of supporting \(\CompilerFrontendCount\) frontends and \(\CompilerBackendCount\) backends to \(\mathcal{O}(\CompilerFrontendCount+ \CompilerBackendCount)\): a single graph capture mechanism (PyTorch’s TorchDynamo, or TensorFlow’s bridge into XLA) can target multiple hardware backends without rewriting the capture logic.

The eager-vs.-compiled trade-off has a direct iron law consequence: JIT compilation amortizes \(\Latency\) (dispatch overhead) and fuses memory traffic across each captured region, so compilation pays only when captured regions are long enough and stable enough to amortize capture, lowering, code generation, and cache-management costs.

Graph-capture JIT systems follow a common architectural pattern across frameworks. The first execution observes tensor operations, records a graph region guarded by assumptions about shapes, dtypes, layouts, and control flow, lowers that region into an intermediate representation, applies fusion and layout optimizations, and caches executable code for later calls that satisfy the same guards. The compiled artifact remains valid only while those guards hold; a failed guard forces the runtime to recompile or fall back to eager execution.

Lowering into an intermediate representation is far easier when the target vocabulary is small, and the eager frameworks a compiler starts from are not small. Figure 6 counts the operators that each framework defines at successive releases. PyTorch’s native-function table grew from 1,064 schemas, overloads included, at version 1.0 in 2018 to 2,658 by version 2.9 in 2025 (PyTorch Contributors 2025). TensorFlow’s operator registry grew from 594 at version 1.0 in 2017 to 1,563 by version 2.20 in 2025 (TensorFlow Developers 2025). Every one of those entries is something a backend must either implement directly or reduce before compilation through two lowering passes. Functionalization rewrites in-place mutations and view aliases into pure operations that return new tensors, which lets a compiler reorder and fuse nodes without corrupting shared storage. Decomposition then expands composite convenience operators into combinations of a much smaller set of primitive operations.

Compiler-facing representations work from far smaller sets, which grew by tens of operators while the eager surfaces grew by thousands. The Core ATen IR, the operator set that PyTorch’s compiler and export paths lower to, holds 192 operators (PyTorch Contributors 2025). The XLA compiler that TensorFlow and JAX both target defines 123 high-level optimizer (HLO) operations (OpenXLA Project 2025). The Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) standard, which carries models between frameworks and runtimes, defines 203 (ONNX Project 2026). JAX starts on the compiler’s side of this divide. Its user-facing operations reduce to about 135 lax primitives (JAX Developers 2025), a set the size of the compiler cores, so a JAX program reaches XLA with little decomposition left to do. The 13–14× gap between an eager surface and its compiler core (for TensorFlow, on its XLA path) is the work that decomposition performs. A new accelerator backend that implements a small core set can run models written against the full eager surface, which is why operator coverage, not peak throughput, often decides whether a model runs on a new device at all.

Reaching one of those compact compiler IRs requires capturing operations from Python first, which brings back the fidelity-generality trade-off from the start of this section. The two capture modes of PyTorch’s TorchScript, now deprecated in favor of capture and export paths such as torch.export (PyTorch Contributors 2026c, 2026b), make that trade-off concrete. Tracing executed a function with example inputs and recorded the tensor operations observed on that path. Scripting instead compiled a restricted, type-annotated subset of Python source, preserving supported branches in the graph at the cost of rejecting unsupported Python objects, library calls, and metaprogramming. Both produced an IR with static types in static single-assignment (SSA) form, where each variable is assigned exactly once, so fusion, constant folding, and dead code elimination could run without the Python interpreter.

PyTorch Contributors. 2026c. TorchScript.
PyTorch Contributors. 2026b. torch.export.

The main limitation of tracing is that a trace records operations from an observed path rather than preserving arbitrary Python control flow. A data-dependent branch can therefore freeze into whichever side the example input took (listing 3).

In this example, converting the tensor condition to a Python Boolean commonly produces a TracerWarning; the resulting trace can still follow the captured branch for later inputs that require the other branch. Trace warnings should therefore be treated as correctness failures when data-dependent Python control flow is present. The same hazard survives in modern graph-capture systems, which is why they guard each captured region with explicit assumptions about shapes and control flow.

Compilation therefore attacks the dispatch overhead of section 7.3.4 directly, and fusion adds a second saving in memory traffic. Notebook 7.1 quantifies both for a two-operation activation block.

Napkin Math 7.1: The physics of software overhead
Iron law connection: For small operations, the latency term \((\Latency)\) in the iron law is dominated by software overhead: dispatching instructions from Python to the GPU. Each operation pays a Python dispatch cost of ~10 μs plus a kernel launch cost of ~5 μs, which together set the per-operation overhead applied in the two scenarios below. Python dispatch covers the framework call; driver launch covers submission to the device. A compiled region invoked from Python still pays an entry cost, whereas replay of a captured launch sequence removes repeated per-operation Python work and has its own submission cost.

Scenario one: Eager mode (the tiny-op trap) Consider a simple activation block, y = relu(x + bias), in which the input and output each contain \(\TensorElementCount\) elements of \(\ElementStorageSize\) bytes each. Assume a scalar or broadcast bias remains in fast memory, so the traffic model counts the full input, intermediate, and output tensors.

  • Operations: Two (Add, ReLU).
  • Execution:
    1. Launch Add Kernel: 15 μs overhead.
    2. Read/Write Memory: \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes.
    3. Launch ReLU Kernel: 15 μs overhead.
    4. Read/Write Memory: \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes.
  • Total overhead: 30 μs.
  • Total memory traffic: \(4\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes.

Scenario two: Compiled mode (fusion) The compiler fuses this into one kernel: FusedAddRelu. This scenario retains one ordinary Python entry and one driver launch, applying the same combined cost as eager mode to isolate the effect of reducing two calls to one. It does not model captured-graph replay.

  • Execution:
    1. Launch Fused Kernel: 15 μs overhead.
    2. Read/Write Memory: \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes (the intermediate result stays on chip).
  • Total overhead: 15 μs (2× less launch overhead).
  • Total memory traffic: \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes (2× bandwidth efficiency).

Systems insight: Fusion saves on both terms at once: half the launches and, by the fused-chain traffic rule of section 7.3.2 with \(\ChainedOperationCount = 2\), half the idealized traffic.

Scaling this arithmetic to a sequence of 5 small operations exposes the idle gaps on the accelerator timeline (figure 7). In eager execution, each 1 μs kernel waits on 10 μs of Python dispatch, leaving the accelerator idle for about 91 percent of the sequence. The figure isolates the Python share, and the per-kernel launch cost would widen each gap further. Compiling the sequence resolves both constraints. The framework submits the work in a single launch of 5 μs, which eliminates all intermediate host dispatch gaps. Operator fusion keeps intermediate activations in registers or cache, so the entire sequence completes in 4 μs instead of the uncompiled 5 μs.

Graph-capture compilers such as PyTorch 2.0’s torch.compile13 (Ansel et al. 2024) realize the compiled timeline of figure 7 over captured regions without requiring engineers to write custom CUDA kernels.14 However, compilation cannot span arbitrary Python code: graph breaks mark the boundary where compilation ceases to apply (a configuration requiring full-graph capture can instead report an error). Data-dependent Python control flow, unsupported library calls, I/O, custom Python objects, and highly variable shapes can shorten compiled regions. With eager fallback enabled, each break separates compiled subgraphs with standard Python execution, reintroducing interpreter dispatch, resetting \(\Latency\) amortization, and preventing fusion across the boundary. Tensor handles can pass between these regions while their storage remains on the device; host reads of device values can additionally require synchronization. This is why graph-break analysis belongs in performance engineering: the relevant metric is not whether compilation is enabled, but how much of the hot path remains inside long, stable compiled regions.

13 torch.compile: PyTorch’s capture front end analyzes Python bytecode as each frame runs, rather than recording tensor operations alone. That vantage lets it keep capturing across Python control flow it can resolve from guarded values and insert a graph break where it cannot, which is why graph breaks rather than tracing errors are its characteristic failure mode.

Ansel, Jason, Edward Yang, Horace He, Natalia Gimelshein, Animesh Jain, Michael Voznesensky, Bin Bao, et al. 2024. “PyTorch 2: Faster Machine Learning Through Dynamic Python Bytecode Transformation and Graph Compilation.” Proceedings of the 29th ACM International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems, Volume 2, 929–47. https://doi.org/10.1145/3620665.3640366.

14 CUDA (compute unified device architecture): NVIDIA’s parallel computing platform, released in 2007, serving as the foundational layer between high-level Python operations and GPU silicon; when PyTorch executes torch.matmul(A, W), the call traverses the framework’s dispatcher, selects a cuBLAS kernel, and launches it on the GPU. Each launch adds the 5 μs kernel-launch share of the per-operation overhead on top of the framework’s Python dispatch. For small operations, this dispatch overhead \((\Latency)\) can exceed useful compute time. Fusing compatible groups of operations then reduces launch count as well as intermediate memory traffic; capturing the graph alone does not imply a single kernel.

Backends occupy different points on the flexibility-performance spectrum. A JIT compiler traces model execution at runtime to generate optimized kernels dynamically, incurring initial warmup latency during the first pass. An ahead-of-time (AOT) compiler instead compiles captured computation before deployment, shifting compilation out of the hot path and enabling a standalone runtime when the export target supports one. A general JIT backend optimizes ordinary training and serving workloads with moderate compilation cost. A specialized inference backend can apply deeper fusion, precision lowering, and autotuning when the deployment target is fixed. An ahead-of-time mobile or embedded runtime removes even more flexibility to gain footprint and predictability. The same rule governs all of them: the narrower the target and the more stable the graph, the more optimization the compiler can safely perform.

Compilation shortens the list of kernels; a second mechanism removes the per-launch cost of whatever list remains. CUDA Graphs record a fixed sequence of kernel launches, with their arguments and memory addresses, once, and then replay the whole sequence with a single submission from the host (NVIDIA Corporation 2026). Replay requires static shapes and fixed buffer addresses, which is why serving systems capture one graph per batch size. For the GPT-2 XL decoding step that opened the chapter, replay turns 576 separate launches into one submission, so the host’s launch rate no longer sets the step time. What remains is the weight read of about 0.9 ms plus the device-side cost of running those kernels back to back, which fusion attacks. Every later decode floor in this book assumes this kind of capture.

NVIDIA Corporation. 2026. CUDA Graphs: CUDA Programming Guide.

The resulting workflow is a systems decision. Rapid prototyping favors eager execution because architecture changes and guard failures make recompilation cost visible. Long training runs and high-volume inference amortize compilation cost over many executions, provided the model has stable shapes and limited graph breaks. Debugging usually starts in eager mode because errors map directly to source code and standard debuggers such as pdb work mid-execution; compilation is reintroduced after the model behavior is correct and the performance bottleneck is measurable.

↩︎ The cost that slow experiment cycles impose on how many candidate models a team can test is established in Where the Hours Go: Quantifying the ML Lifecycle.

Set side by side across six dimensions (table 2), the three execution models differ most in when the graph is built and how much of it a compiler can see. Each row highlights how a design choice at graph construction time propagates into control flow, debugging, and runtime throughput.

The same divide extends to memory planning, hardware specialization, research velocity, and integration with existing code (table 3). Where static capture invests upfront in planned buffer lifetimes and target-specific code generation, dynamic execution prioritizes rapid iteration and seamless composition with ordinary host code.

These trade-offs are not binary choices. Modern frameworks offer a spectrum of execution options, which raises the quantitative question of where on this spectrum a given project should operate.

7.3.7 Compilation and dispatch costs

Two quantitative principles make the choice of execution model measurable. The compilation continuum principle asks when compilation gains justify development cost by comparing production executions with development iterations. The dispatch overhead law writes the dispatch overhead ratio in terms of operation count and per-operation cost, giving a threshold that says when a whole workload is overhead-bound.

The compilation continuum principle

Equation 1 orders the execution models from maximum flexibility to maximum optimization in four positions, and each labeled arrow names the mechanism that carries a project one step rightward toward hardware: \[ \text{Eager} \xrightarrow{\text{capture}} \text{Graph JIT} \xrightarrow{\text{export}} \text{AOT Runtime} \xrightarrow{\text{specialize}} \text{Hardware} \tag{1}\] Each step rightward sacrifices flexibility for performance. The practical question is where a given project should be placed on this continuum. The optimal compilation strategy depends on production executions, development recompilations, and their respective time costs, combined in equation 2:

\[ \text{Compilation Benefit} = \frac{\ProductionExecutionCount\cdot (\EagerExecutionTime- \CompiledExecutionTime)}{\CompilationTime+ \DevelopmentIterationCount\cdot \CompilationTime} \tag{2}\]

where:

  • \(\ProductionExecutionCount\) = number of production executions (dimensionless count: inference requests, training steps)
  • \(\DevelopmentIterationCount\) = number of development iterations requiring recompilation (dimensionless count)
  • \(\EagerExecutionTime\) = time per execution in eager mode (seconds)
  • \(\CompiledExecutionTime\) = time per execution in compiled mode (seconds)
  • \(\CompilationTime\) = time per compilation or recompilation (seconds; assumed constant)

This model assumes that the compiled artifact remains valid between development changes and that the initial compilation and each recompilation have the same cost; under these assumptions, the decision rule is to compile when \(\text{Compilation Benefit} > 1\). The ratio is dimensionless.

Table 4 applies that decision rule to hypothetical throughputs and compilation times across execution modes and model architectures:

Three cases illustrate how reuse changes the compilation decision. In research prototyping (\(\DevelopmentIterationCount\gg \ProductionExecutionCount\)), teams should stay eager. If the architecture changes every few minutes, compilation overhead can dominate. Under the scenario’s 30 s compilation assumption, 10 compilations per hour consume 5 min.

For repeated execution (\(\ProductionExecutionCount\gg \DevelopmentIterationCount\)), compilation can pay off because its one-time cost is amortized across many calls. In the hypothetical ResNet-50 inference scenario in table 4, torch.compile provides 48.3 percent higher throughput (2,150 img/s vs. 1,450 img/s). Using the scenario’s 30 s compile cost, this pays off after the breakeven point in equation 3:

\[ \CompilationBreakevenCount= \frac{\CompilationTime}{\EagerExecutionTime- \CompiledExecutionTime} \tag{3}\]

Evaluating equation 3 with the hypothetical ResNet-50 values gives approximately 134,000 images. The same amortization principle applies to long training runs, using training throughput and compilation costs measured for that workload.

Long-lived production inference (\(\DevelopmentIterationCount\approx 0\), \(\ProductionExecutionCount\rightarrow \infty\)) extends this reuse further, providing more opportunity to amortize compilation. Aggressive autotuning can be worthwhile even when setup takes much longer, provided the model converts correctly, meets numerical requirements, and improves measured performance over the deployment lifetime.

The cases form a continuum of increasing reuse (figure 8). Each strategy’s total time starts at its one-time compilation cost and grows at its per-execution cost. Eager therefore wins at low execution counts, JIT wins once its compilation is amortized, and AOT wins only at the largest counts, where its higher upfront cost is fully repaid. The figure’s crossovers (near \(2 \times 10^{6}\) and \(6.7 \times 10^{6}\) executions) come from an illustrative cost model in arbitrary units, not from the ResNet-50 scenario, whose eager-to-compiled breakeven sits near 134,000 images. What carries over is the order of the regions, not their positions.

These throughput differences are not uniform across workloads, so the execution strategy must align with the operational regime of each workload archetype. The appropriate strategy depends on the computational phase, the target backend, and the dominant iron law term. In batched prefill, transformers have high arithmetic intensity that benefits from dense matrix compilation; in small-batch autoregressive decoding, however, the workload shifts into a bandwidth-bound regime where memory transfers dominate. Table 5 maps each recurring archetype to a candidate execution strategy based on these primary physical constraints.

↩︎ The workload archetypes and the iron law term that dominates each are established in Workload archetypes.

The rows differ in what a compiler can reach. Dense convolution exposes most of its work to fusion and layout selection, while embedding gathers and a microcontroller’s fixed memory sit largely outside the graph a compiler optimizes.

Lighthouse 7.1: Framework strategy by archetype
The four recurring archetypes in table 5 trace one principle across the framework: compilation benefits scale with how much of the workload is optimizable.

Systems insight: In the illustrative scenario, compute-bound workloads such as the ResNet-50 row in table 4 benefit because their dense kernels expose substantial surface for layout selection, precision lowering, and fusion. Bandwidth-bound workloads such as small-batch GPT-2 generation require distinguishing intermediate activation traffic from parameter streaming. Fusing attention kernels eliminates round-trips for transient activation tensors and removes dispatch latency, but memory bandwidth continues to bind on streaming model weights and key-value caches unless batching increases arithmetic intensity. Capacity-bound workloads such as DLRM can gain less when irregular embedding lookups leave limited compiler scope.

The dispatch overhead law

Whereas the compilation continuum asks whether enough executions will run to repay compilation setup, the dispatch overhead law asks whether a single execution is stalled on the host in the first place. Let \(\DispatchedOperationCount\) be the number of operations (count), \(\OperationDispatchOverhead\) the per-operation dispatch overhead (seconds), and \(\IronLawComputeTime\) and \(\IronLawMemoryTime\) the total compute and memory times (seconds). Under the additive, no-overlap approximation \(\DeviceHardwareTime\approx \IronLawComputeTime + \IronLawMemoryTime\) (when execution overlaps those costs, measured hardware time should replace their sum), equation 4 identifies when framework overhead dominates compute and memory:

\[ \text{Overhead Ratio} = \frac{\DispatchedOperationCount\cdot \OperationDispatchOverhead}{\IronLawComputeTime + \IronLawMemoryTime} \tag{4}\]

When Overhead Ratio \(> 1\), the model is overhead-bound. This is the dispatch overhead ratio \(\HostSoftwareTime/\DeviceHardwareTime\) of section 7.3.4, written with \(\HostSoftwareTime= \DispatchedOperationCount\cdot \OperationDispatchOverhead\) so that operation count and per-operation cost appear explicitly. The fraction of step time spent in dispatch is \(\text{Overhead Ratio}/(1+\text{Overhead Ratio})\), so a ratio above 1 means dispatch takes more than half of the step. Compilation can provide its largest gains for overhead-bound workloads because fusion reduces the number of dispatches. The training-step trace in section 7.7 works this effect through end to end, previewed by the numbers that follow.

Applying equation 4 means comparing the software overhead \(\HostSoftwareTime= \DispatchedOperationCount\cdot \OperationDispatchOverhead\) with the hardware execution time \(\DeviceHardwareTime\) (both in seconds). The regime depends on the size of individual operations rather than on the model’s parameter count. Notebook 7.2 contrasts a small multilayer perceptron with a large transformer layer to quantify when host overhead or device arithmetic bounds the system.

Napkin Math 7.2: Dispatch overhead at two scales
Problem: Determine the threshold where host-side Python interpreter dispatch overhead bounds end-to-end execution time relative to accelerator hardware execution.

Scenario one: Small multilayer perceptron (overhead bound)

  • Compute: 6 ops across small matrix/element-wise operations.
  • Hardware time: \(\DeviceHardwareTime\approx\) 2.6 μs (mostly memory latency).
  • Software overhead: \(\HostSoftwareTime\approx\) 6 ops \(\times\) 15 μs/op = 90 μs.
  • Ratio: 90 μs/2.6 μs ≈ 34.6.
  • Small-model outcome: The system spends 97 percent of time in host-side dispatch and kernel-launch overhead. Eliminating all modeled dispatch overhead would give an upper-bound speedup of 35.6×.

Scenario two: GPT-3 layer (device bound)

  • Compute: Huge matrix multiplications.
  • Assumptions: Both times below are illustrative round values for one GPT-3-scale layer over a large batch, not derived from a specific accelerator. The conclusion does not hinge on them, because at the chapter’s 15 μs/op the ratio would reach 1 only if the layer issued thousands of separately dispatched kernels.
  • Hardware time: \(\DeviceHardwareTime\approx\) 100 ms = 100000 μs.
  • Software overhead: \(\HostSoftwareTime \approx 180 \, \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), one transformer block’s 12 eager kernels at 15 μs/op.
  • Ratio: 180 μs/100000 μs ≈ 0.0018.
  • Large-model outcome: Modeled Python overhead is negligible. Compilation may still improve fusion, layout, or backend specialization; dispatch elimination contributes little.

Systems insight: Dispatch overhead is regime-dependent. Compilation can reduce host-side overhead for small-operation workloads by fusing operations into fewer launches, while large models benefit mainly from fused kernels and memory movement reductions.

7.3.8 Static runtimes for microcontrollers

The compilation continuum ends at the far edge, where the binding cost on a microcontroller is not each dispatch but the runtime itself, whose memory and latency footprint exceeds the device. While cloud frameworks such as PyTorch and TensorFlow 2.x prioritize flexibility through eager execution, TinyML15 systems operating on microcontrollers (MCUs) with kilobytes of memory cannot afford the overhead of a Python interpreter or a fully dynamic runtime.

15 TinyML: Systems designed for MCUs cannot afford a Python interpreter. Micro-runtimes instead use small C/C++ runtimes, fixed memory planning, and model-specific operators. TensorFlow Lite Micro remains interpreter-based: the application supplies a tensor arena whose buffers are planned and reused without heap allocation after setup. Predictable memory is mandatory because exceeding a 256 KB device budget can prevent initialization or inference.

Lighthouse 7.2: Lighthouse example: KWS on TinyML
Scenario: Deploying the keyword spotting (KWS) lighthouse, a DS-CNN and the book’s power-bound workload, to an Nordic nRF52840 (Cortex-M4F) microcontroller with 256 KB RAM and 1 MB Flash. This is a smaller target than the ESP32-S3 of the earlier smart-doorbell scenario, chosen to show the runtime at its tightest.

Constraint: A full PyTorch and Python runtime exceeds the device’s memory budget by orders of magnitude.

Framework solution: Micro-frameworks such as TensorFlow Lite Micro (TFLM) (David et al. 2021) solve this through a tiny interpreter-based runtime with a fixed memory discipline:

  1. Fixed memory arena: The application supplies a contiguous tensor arena, and the framework plans and reuses buffers from that arena rather than relying on dynamic allocation during inference.
  2. Kernel selection: Only the specific kernels used by the model (for example, Conv2D, DepthwiseConv) need to be linked or registered with the runtime.
  3. Compact interpreter execution: The MCU runs a small C/C++ interpreter over a flat model representation, with the model and arena bound at initialization rather than assembled dynamically at runtime.

Binding constraint: The power budget makes keyword spotting a power-bound workload, but the first gate the framework must pass is memory capacity, not bandwidth or dispatch overhead, which is why table 5 treats it as a feasibility gate. The framework’s primary job is to ensure the model’s intermediate activations (the “working set”) fit within the MCU’s tiny SRAM.

David, Robert, Jared Duke, Advait Jain, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Nat Jeffries, Jian Li, Nick Kreeger, et al. 2021. “TensorFlow Lite Micro: Embedded Machine Learning for TinyML Systems.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 3: 800–811.

These micro-runtimes sacrifice dynamic flexibility to reduce footprint and make memory predictable. With compact models, low-power hardware, and duty cycling, that discipline enables milliwatt-scale inference with mostly on-chip data movement. Across the full spectrum from dynamic eager execution to static graph compilation and specialized micro-runtimes, developers must therefore match the execution strategy to the binding constraint of the target.

Checkpoint 7.1: Execution models

The choice of execution mode determines both developer velocity and model performance.

Debuggability vs. speed

Modern compromise

The execution problem determines when computation happens and what optimizations are possible. Neural network training, however, requires a capability that no amount of clever scheduling can provide: the ability to compute gradients automatically.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A GPU performance profile reveals that a sequence of LayerNorm, dropout, and GELU activation operations spends over 80% of its execution time reading and writing intermediate tensors to High Bandwidth Memory (HBM) with very low arithmetic intensity. Why is operator fusion the primary framework optimization for this workload?

    1. It replaces 16-bit floating-point arithmetic with 8-bit integer arithmetic
    2. It changes the model architecture to eliminate all non-linear activation functions
    3. It converts compute-bound matrix multiplications into memory-bound operations
    4. It fuses multiple sequential element-wise operations into a single GPU kernel, keeping intermediate values in on-chip SRAM/registers and eliminating redundant round trips to HBM

  2. A developer traces a PyTorch model containing data-dependent control flow such as if tensor.sum() > 0: ... using torch.jit.trace, then later runs inputs that should take the other branch. What is the most accurate description of the tracing risk and the modern graph-capture behavior described in the section?

    1. Tracing always crashes immediately on tensor-dependent branches, whereas modern graph capture always compiles the entire program without fallback
    2. Tracing converts the Python conditional into a fully general static graph branch, whereas modern graph capture removes the need for any control-flow assumptions
    3. Tracing records the branch taken by the example input and may emit a TracerWarning, while modern graph capture can compile supported regions and, when eager fallback is enabled, return to eager execution at unsupported boundaries
    4. Tracing silently succeeds for all future inputs, whereas modern graph capture fails only when the GPU lacks enough memory

  3. State the dispatch overhead law and explain why a workload composed of many small tensor operations on a fast GPU can be severely underutilized in eager mode even when the hardware has high peak throughput.

  4. True or False: When eager fallback is enabled in torch.compile, encountering a graph break necessarily halts execution with a runtime error because the unsupported Python code cannot be represented in the captured graph.

  5. Order the stages of a generic graph-capture compilation pipeline from the first Python call to repeated execution of optimized code: (1) an intermediate representation is built for the captured region, (2) optimized executable code is cached for reuse under the same guards, (3) the frontend captures a region of tensor computation from the eager program, (4) the compiled region executes on the target hardware, (5) optimization and lowering transform the captured region toward hardware-specific execution.

  6. In graph-capture JIT compilation systems like TorchDynamo, an event where the compiler encounters an unsupported dynamic Python construct (such as an unhandled C-extension call or dynamic side effect) and must pause graph capture to yield control to the Python interpreter is called a ____.

7.4 Differentiation: Automatic Gradient Computation

A framework that executes graphs efficiently but cannot compute gradients16 can run inference, yet it cannot learn. Training requires the derivative of a scalar loss \(\LossFunction\) with respect to every parameter: doing this manually for even a three-layer network demands dozens of hand-derived partial derivatives, and for a billion-parameter transformer it is intractable. The differentiation problem is the task of mechanizing that derivative computation automatically. During the forward pass, the runtime records an execution tape and saves intermediate activation buffers for subsequent derivative evaluations. As backpropagation already showed, reverse-mode automatic differentiation (AD)17 matches this regime because a single reverse traversal from a scalar loss yields every parameter gradient (Baydin et al. 2018).

16 Gradients: A gradient holds one value for every parameter it differentiates, so each weight tensor acquires a gradient tensor of the same shape. Computing gradients automatically therefore doubles the parameter-sized state before any optimizer runs, which is why training-memory accounts, such as the one in Worked example: Training vs. inference memory, list gradients as a full copy of the weights.

17 Reverse-mode AD: Reverse mode’s single backward pass has a concrete memory cost, because the operation-dependent values that derivative rules read must be retained until the backward traversal reaches them. Activation checkpointing changes this saved set by omitting selected values and recomputing them during the backward pass.

Baydin, Atilim Gunes, Barak A. Pearlmutter, Alexey Andreyevich Radul, and Jeffrey Mark Siskind. 2018. “Automatic Differentiation in Machine Learning: A Survey.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 18 (153): 1–43.

The systems engineering of differentiation extends the backpropagation algorithm. It addresses how frameworks represent computation graphs, manage memory for intermediate values, and orchestrate the backward pass efficiently across accelerators. The framework mechanizes the chain rule as large-scale systems bookkeeping: tracking operator dependencies, staging activation buffers, and dispatching backward kernels. The function in listing 4 reduces the core idea to three elementary operations.

↪ The optimization algorithms that consume these gradients are developed in Optimizers and their memory.

Frameworks decompose this function into elementary operations, each with a known local derivative, and then combine these local derivatives via the chain rule to compute gradients through supported compositions. The systems challenge is executing this transformation under physical hardware constraints: the framework must represent the computation graph, make necessary forward values available, and schedule backward kernels without exhausting accelerator HBM. Production runtimes address these challenges through structured graph recording, caching allocators, and reverse-mode traversal.

7.4.1 Forward and reverse mode

Computing the gradient of a scalar loss across millions of model weights one weight at a time would require millions of separate passes through the network, whereas sweeping backward from the single scalar loss recovers every weight gradient in one reverse pass. Automatic differentiation operates in these two traversal directions: forward mode, whose cost scales with the number of inputs, and reverse mode, whose cost scales with the number of outputs. Because deep learning objectives reduce a large parameter set to a single scalar loss, this asymmetry dictates both the memory footprint and the execution schedule of the underlying framework.

Forward mode

Forward mode automatic differentiation computes directional derivatives alongside the forward calculation itself. For each intermediate variable \(\TraceIntermediateVariable_i\), the runtime evaluates both the value \(\TraceIntermediateVariable_i\) and its directional derivative (tangent) \(\TraceIntermediateTangent_i = \sum_j \frac{\partial \TraceIntermediateVariable_i}{\partial \FunctionInputVariable_j} \FunctionInputTangent_j\). Because tangents flow forward alongside the values, forward mode requires no execution tape and never retains forward activations for a later backward pass.

To see the mechanism concretely, consider computing both the value and derivative of \(\IllustrativeFunction(\FunctionInputVariable) = \FunctionInputVariable^2 \sin(\FunctionInputVariable)\). Listing 5 shows how forward mode evaluates local derivative rules alongside each elementary operation, applying the chain and product rules at every step:

Forward mode mechanizes this paired tracking through dual numbers, values of the form \(\TraceIntermediateVariable+ \TraceIntermediateTangent\DualNumberInfinitesimal\) where \(\DualNumberInfinitesimal^2 = 0\), so each operator updates the value and its tangent together without retaining a graph. Seeding \(\FunctionInputTangent= 1.0\) carries the derivative through the trace (listing 6).

A single forward pass with a seeded tangent vector \(\TangentSeedVector\) evaluates a Jacobian-vector product (JVP), \(\FunctionJacobian\TangentSeedVector\), where \(\FunctionJacobian\in \mathbb{R}^{\FunctionOutputDimension\times \Parameters}\) is the Jacobian matrix. When an objective maps \(\Parameters\) parameter inputs to a single scalar loss (\(\FunctionOutputDimension= 1\)), recovering the full gradient \(\nabla_{\ModelParameters}\LossFunction \in \mathbb{R}^{\Parameters}\) requires setting \(\TangentSeedVector\) to each of the \(\Parameters\) standard basis vectors in turn. Recovering gradients with forward mode therefore requires \(\Parameters\) distinct forward evaluations (or propagating \(\Parameters\)-wide tangent bundles), scaling compute and memory traffic as \(\mathcal{O}(\Parameters)\) with parameter count. For networks with millions or billions of parameters, this scaling makes forward-mode gradient calculation intractable, although forward JVPs remain effective when input dimensions are small or when evaluating directional derivatives and Hessian-vector products.

Reverse mode

An intermediate layer that maps an input vector \(\FunctionInputVector\in \mathbb{R}^\FunctionInputDimension\) to an output \(\FunctionOutputVector\in \mathbb{R}^\FunctionOutputDimension\) of width \(\FunctionInputDimension= \FunctionOutputDimension= 100{,}000\) has a full Jacobian \(\FunctionJacobian\in \mathbb{R}^{\FunctionOutputDimension\times \FunctionInputDimension}\) of \(10^{10}\) elements, which would require 40 GB of memory in FP32 to store one layer’s derivatives. Reverse-mode automatic differentiation avoids materializing this matrix and matches the many-parameters-to-one-loss shape of neural network training. Instead of propagating tangents forward from inputs, reverse mode propagates adjoints \(\TraceIntermediateAdjoint_i = \partial \LossFunction / \partial \TraceIntermediateVariable_i\) backward from the single scalar loss \(\LossFunction\) to all parameters.

While forward mode computes Jacobian-vector products (\(\FunctionJacobian\TangentSeedVector\)), reverse mode evaluates vector-Jacobian products (VJPs), \(\UpstreamGradientVector^T \FunctionJacobian= \sum_{j=1}^\FunctionOutputDimension \UpstreamGradientElement_j \, \partial \FunctionOutputElement_j / \partial \FunctionInputVector\), where \(\UpstreamGradientElement_j = \partial \LossFunction / \partial \FunctionOutputElement_j\) is one element of the upstream gradient vector \(\UpstreamGradientVector= \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \FunctionOutputVector} \in \mathbb{R}^{\FunctionOutputDimension}\). Because training optimizes a single scalar loss \(\LossFunction\), the backward pass only ever needs to multiply this upstream gradient vector by each local operation’s derivative rule, never the 40 GB full Jacobian matrix.

To see how VJPs sidestep the full matrix, consider a simple element-wise operation such as \(\FunctionOutputVector= \text{ReLU}(\FunctionInputVector)\) with an input vector \(\FunctionInputVector= [-1.0, 2.0, 3.0]\) and an upstream gradient \(\UpstreamGradientVector= [0.5, 0.5, 0.5]\) flowing backward from the loss. Because the derivative of ReLU is \(1\) when \(\FunctionInputVariable> 0\) and \(0\) otherwise, the local gradient vector is \(\frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVector}{\partial \FunctionInputVector} = [0, 1, 1]\). Instead of building a \(3{\times}3\) diagonal matrix and executing a matrix multiplication, the VJP computes the element-wise product \(\UpstreamGradientVector\odot \frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVector}{\partial \FunctionInputVector} = [0.0, 0.5, 0.5]\) directly. It streams vectors without ever materializing the full \(\FunctionOutputDimension\times \FunctionInputDimension\) matrix in memory. Seeding the reverse sweep with \(\LossAdjointSeed = 1\) yields the complete parameter gradient vector \(\nabla_{\ModelParameters}\LossFunction\) in a single backward pass, regardless of whether the model contains ten parameters or ten billion.

Reverse mode operates on the same three-operation function \(\IllustrativeFunction(\FunctionInputVariable) = \FunctionInputVariable^2\sin(\FunctionInputVariable)\) from listing 4, where \(\FunctionInputVariable\) reaches the output through two distinct computational paths: the square and the sine. Algorithm 1 defines the reverse-mode traversal algorithm over a directed acyclic graph before tracing this concrete evaluation.

\(y\)

The algorithmic structure of algorithm 1 reveals its systems cost. The forward pass records operator dependencies and buffers intermediate values required by backward derivative rules until the reverse sweep consumes them. Reverse mode trades activation storage and graph bookkeeping for a single backward pass, whose cost is a small constant multiple of the forward pass regardless of parameter count (Baur and Strassen 1983; Griewank and Walther 2008).

Baur, Walter, and Volker Strassen. 1983. “The Complexity of Partial Derivatives.” Theoretical Computer Science 22 (3): 317–30. https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-3975(83)90110-x.
Griewank, Andreas, and Andrea Walther. 2008. Evaluating Derivatives: Principles and Techniques of Algorithmic Differentiation. 2nd ed. SIAM. https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9780898717761.

For the concrete function in listing 4 evaluated at \(\FunctionInputVariable=2.0\), the forward pass records \(\WorkedTraceSquare=\FunctionInputVariable^2=4.0\), \(\WorkedTraceSine=\sin(\FunctionInputVariable)\approx0.9093\), and \(\WorkedTraceProduct=\WorkedTraceSquare \WorkedTraceSine\approx3.637\). Seeding \(\WorkedTraceProductAdjoint=1.0\), the product rule yields adjoints \(\WorkedTraceSquareAdjoint=0.9093\) and \(\WorkedTraceSineAdjoint=4.0\). The square branch contributes \(0.9093\cdot4.0\approx3.6372\) to \(\FunctionInputAdjoint\), while the sine branch contributes \(4.0\cos(2.0)\approx-1.6646\). Accumulating both paths gives the exact derivative \(\partial \WorkedTraceProduct/\partial \FunctionInputVariable= \FunctionInputAdjoint\approx 1.973\).

This single backward traversal computes \(\partial \WorkedTraceProduct/\partial \FunctionInputVariable\) regardless of how many fan-out paths connect \(\FunctionInputVariable\) to \(\WorkedTraceProduct\). In a deep neural network, every weight tensor influences the scalar loss through thousands of divergent paths across successive layers; reverse mode aggregates all incoming adjoints in one reverse sweep. That one sweep, rather than one pass per parameter, is what keeps training a 175B-parameter model such as GPT-3 computationally tractable.

Executing this traversal in hardware exposes a physical constraint: evaluating local derivatives during the backward pass requires the operating point established during forward execution. The framework must therefore determine which forward tensors to persist in device memory, when to allocate them, and when to free them. The recorded graph answers the “when to free” question, because each saved tensor can be released as soon as the last backward rule that reads it has run. The peak itself is bounded separately, by splitting a large batch into micro-batches whose saved activations are freed before the next one starts while their gradients sum into the same buffers (gradient accumulation).18

18 Gradient accumulation: The framework processes a large logical batch as smaller micro-batches, freeing each micro-batch’s saved activations after backward while accumulating parameter-sized gradients. Processing 4,096 samples in 64-sample micro-batches can reduce the batch-dependent activation peak by up to 64\(\times\) when activation memory scales linearly with batch size; weights, gradients, optimizer state, and other fixed costs do not shrink. The trade-off is more sequential work before each optimizer update.

A two-layer network makes these requirements concrete (listing 7). Tracing both passes explicitly shows which forward activations must survive until the reverse sweep consumes them.

Three implementation requirements emerge from this example. First, the framework must track dependencies between operations to determine the correct reverse traversal order. Second, the information needed by each backward rule must be available, either saved during the forward pass or regenerated. The chain rule evaluates local derivatives at the forward operating point. For example, evaluating \(\frac{\partial}{\partial \NeuronPreactivation}\text{ReLU}(\NeuronPreactivation) = \IndicatorFunction(\NeuronPreactivation> 0)\) requires the sign of the pre-activation \(\NeuronPreactivation\), which can be recovered from a saved value or mask. Computing a dense layer’s weight gradient \(\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \WeightMatrix} = \LayerInputMatrix^T \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \PreactivationMatrix}\), where \(\PreactivationMatrix\) is the layer’s pre-activation output, requires the forward input activation tensor \(\LayerInputMatrix\). Third, every differentiated operation needs both a forward implementation and a corresponding backward rule. These requirements define the engineering surface of automatic differentiation, where retaining forward values can consume much of the training memory budget.

7.4.2 Activation memory

Retaining those forward values turns device memory capacity, rather than raw floating-point throughput, into the feasibility boundary of training. A 175B-parameter model in FP16 already requires 350 GB for weights alone, exceeding the physical memory of any single accelerator before a single forward activation is saved, and reverse-mode AD compounds that pressure by retaining operation-dependent activations for backward derivative rules. Saved activations weigh just as heavily on models whose weights fit easily: in a batch-64 ResNet-50 (worked in section 7.4.3.2), saved activations exceed the FP32 weights by roughly two orders of magnitude, on top of weights, gradients, and optimizer states.

This saved-activation footprint accumulates across successive layers, because each forward layer adds tensors that must persist until the backward sweep unwinds to that layer (listing 8). For a batch of \(\BatchSize\) sequences of length \(\SequenceLength\) passing through \(\LayerCount\) layers of width \(\HiddenDimension\), each saving \(\LayerSavedTensorCount\) tensors of \(\ElementStorageSize\) bytes per element, the saved state is approximately \[ \ActivationMemory \approx \BatchSize \times \LayerCount \times \SequenceLength \times \HiddenDimension \times \LayerSavedTensorCount\times \ElementStorageSize \] This total grows linearly in both batch size and network depth. Consequently, the batch size that fits on an accelerator during training is far smaller than the batch size that fits during inference, where intermediate activations can be discarded as soon as the next layer consumes them.

This is a capacity limit rather than the bandwidth gap of the memory wall, and frameworks relieve it through two primary architectural strategies. The first is activation checkpointing (or gradient checkpointing), a reverse-mode technique (Griewank and Walther 2000) that deep learning frameworks adopted to train deep networks in sublinear memory (Chen et al. 2016). Rather than buffering every intermediate activation, the runtime stores only selected segment boundary tensors and recomputes dropped intermediates on demand during the backward pass. In listing 9, the backward pass re-executes each forward segment to regenerate its activations rather than loading them from global memory.

Griewank, Andreas, and Andrea Walther. 2000. “Algorithm 799: Revolve: An Implementation of Checkpointing for the Reverse or Adjoint Mode of Computational Differentiation.” ACM Transactions on Mathematical Software 26 (1): 19–45. https://doi.org/10.1145/347837.347846.
Chen, Tianqi, Bing Xu, Chiyuan Zhang, and Carlos Guestrin. 2016. “Training Deep Nets with Sublinear Memory Cost.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1604.06174.

↪ Activation checkpointing schedules and their recomputation cost are developed in Optimal checkpoint placement strategy.

The second strategy is the kernel fusion that the memory wall motivated in section 7.3.2, applied here to the body of a layer. Its capacity benefit is narrower than its traffic benefit. Temporaries that pass only between the fused operators are never allocated in HBM, so they add nothing to peak memory, while any value a backward rule reads must still be stored.19 Rather than executing matrix multiplication, bias addition, and activation functions as separate kernels that serialize intermediate tensors to accelerator global memory (HBM), compilers fuse compatible operators into a single kernel that passes intermediate values through registers or on-chip shared memory.

19 Fusion and saved state: Fusion removes the forward round-trip of an intermediate through HBM, but it does not remove the backward pass’s need for that value. If a derivative rule reads a fused intermediate, such as the pre-activation whose sign forms the ReLU mask, the fused kernel must still write that value (or a compact mask) to memory, or the backward pass must recompute it. Fusion and checkpointing therefore interact, because the compiler decides which intermediates to materialize from what the backward graph will read.

Chetlur, Sharan, Cliff Woolley, Philippe Vandermersch, Jonathan Cohen, John Tran, Bryan Catanzaro, and Evan Shelhamer. 2014. “cuDNN: Efficient Primitives for Deep Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1410.0759.

The backward pass itself relies on specialized hardware-optimized kernels. Rather than synthesizing backward passes from naive elementary operations, frameworks invoke tuned backward kernels (such as cuDNN primitives) that optimize memory access strides and register usage on accelerator hardware (Chetlur et al. 2014). Combining checkpointing, kernel fusion, and specialized backward kernels enables large architectures to train within physical accelerator memory limits without incurring out-of-memory faults.

7.4.3 Autograd internals

Checkpointing, fusion, and specialized kernels address the physical bottlenecks of automatic differentiation, but production frameworks hide these mechanisms beneath a training loop of four calls. These calls clear the gradients, run the forward pass, call backward on the loss, and step the optimizer. Behind those calls, the runtime builds and traverses a dynamic computation graph, allocates and frees intermediate buffers, schedules backward kernels on device streams, and applies a numerical-precision policy.

Recording the backward graph

If a forward operation discards its inputs and leaves no record of what function produced its output, calling loss.backward() has no path back to the model weights and no saved activations to plug into the chain rule. To make reverse traversal possible without ahead-of-time graph compilation, the autograd system dynamically constructs a directed acyclic graph of Node (or Function) objects during the forward pass. Each node stores an operator’s backward differentiation rule and holds references to the tensors needed to evaluate its vector-Jacobian products. Because edges point backward from outputs toward inputs, the engine can walk the graph in reverse topological order as soon as it receives the initial gradient from the scalar loss. The memory that those node references keep alive is examined in section 7.4.3.2.

When gradient recording is enabled and an operation has inputs requiring gradients, its output carries a grad_fn referencing the backward Function. Each Function links to antecedent nodes through its next_functions list, forming a chain from the scalar loss back to leaf parameter tensors. Listing 10 inspects this pointer chain for a simple composite computation:

The inspection exposes the traversal path: PowBackward0 (from z = y.pow(2)) links to MulBackward0 (from y = x * 3), which terminates at AccumulateGrad on the leaf parameter tensor x. Leaf tensors are graph sinks where accumulated gradients write directly into the .grad buffer rather than propagating further. The tuple format (Function, index) tracks the specific output index for multi-output operations.

This reverse-linked graph imposes a lifetime constraint: the topology and the forward information required by each backward rule must remain available until that rule executes. Host-side graph metadata (pointers and node descriptors) is distinct from the device tensor buffers it references. Retaining those buffers can consume gigabytes of accelerator capacity as model depth and batch size grow; recomputation or offloading changes where and how the required values are made available.

Production training also needs control over gradient propagation beyond a standard backward sweep, in three forms. Selective gradient computation freezes layers during transfer learning via requires_grad=False or .detach(). Gradient inspection and modification supports clipping, debugging, or logging intermediate adjoints via runtime hooks. Custom differentiation rules cover non-standard operations such as specialized CUDA kernels or novel activation functions. These extension points operate via callbacks invoked during graph construction and reverse traversal, preserving the core engine’s execution path. In PyTorch, these capabilities map to five runtime mechanisms, each with its own memory or correctness hazard (table 6).

Custom autograd functions move two decisions from the engine to the programmer, what to save and how to differentiate. The implementation explicitly defines which forward tensors to preserve in ctx.save_for_backward() and evaluates the corresponding vector-Jacobian products in backward(). Listing 11 demonstrates this interface for a custom multiply-add operation:

Saved tensors and the memory-compute trade-off

Every call to save_for_backward in the backward graph keeps a device tensor live from the forward pass until its backward consumer finishes. Because different operations require different forward information (a matrix multiplication weight gradient requires the full input activation tensor, whereas elementwise ReLU needs only the pre-activation sign), the resulting memory profile depends on each operator’s backward rule and dwarfs the model weights even on modest architectures.

The training memory sum dwarfs FP32 weight storage alone.

Consider an illustrative ResNet-50 training scenario with 25.6M parameters (~102.2 MB of FP32 weights), batch size 64, and \(224{\times}224\) images. With 8 GB–12 GB of saved activations, gradients add ~102.2 MB, and two Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) FP32 moment buffers add ~204.5 MB. Summing those four components gives an 8.4 GB–12.4 GB training scenario, roughly 82.3–121.4× the FP32 weight storage alone. This is a capacity-planning sum; actual peak memory depends on which components are live together. Inference requires no optimizer states, gradients, or saved backward buffers, but still has live activations and runtime workspace.

Parameter count alone therefore does not tell an engineer whether training fits. Total training memory obeys the four-component formulation (\(\TotalMemoryFootprint = \WeightMemory + \GradientMemory + \OptimizerStateMemory + \ActivationMemory\)) plus allocator overhead, as stated in Training memory.

Frameworks provide three graph-level controls to manage this trade-off. Activation checkpointing, whose mechanism listing 9 showed, alters what the graph retains: the engine stores selected segment boundaries and regenerates intermediate activations during the backward pass. In iron law terms, checkpointing increases the arithmetic operation count (\(\Operations\)) to reduce peak activation storage; its effect on transfer volume (\(\DataVolume\)) depends on the recomputation path. Tensor detachment sets a gradient boundary: calling .detach() makes subsequent operations treat that tensor as independent of its prior computation.
