7.8 Major Frameworks: TensorFlow, PyTorch, and JAX

A team that prototypes quickly but cannot deploy the resulting model, or deploys reliably but cannot debug training failures, has run into a framework design trade-off rather than a missing API call. Each major framework solves all three core problems but emphasizes a different path through them. TensorFlow couples eager development to graph capture and a broad deployment ecosystem. PyTorch begins with eager execution and adds capture, compilation, and export paths. JAX organizes differentiation, vectorization, and compilation as composable function transformations. These are architectural emphases, not exclusive capabilities, and their value depends on the workload and deployment target.

7.8.1 TensorFlow

TensorFlow’s architecture spans eager development and graph-based optimization across hardware from cloud TPUs to microcontrollers. TensorFlow 2 executes eagerly by default. Performance-sensitive functions can be captured with tf.function, and deployment formats route captured computation to server, mobile, and browser runtimes. This dual path preserves the production graph capabilities inherited from TensorFlow 1.x without requiring every TensorFlow 2 program to build a complete graph before it runs.

Within a captured tf.function, TensorFlow can apply graph optimizations such as constant folding, kernel fusion, and layout planning. The SavedModel serialization format is the boundary that separates training from deployment (figure 14). Training workflows on the left, including tf.data preprocessing, the Keras modeling API, and distribution strategies across CPUs, GPUs, and TPUs, serialize into one SavedModel artifact. From this central interface, graph definitions pass through supported serving or target-specific conversion paths into deployment runtimes on the right (TensorFlow Serving, TensorFlow Lite, TensorFlow.js, and native language bindings) without retaining Python runtime dependencies.

TensorFlow’s answer to the execution problem is therefore two-sided: eager by default for development, with tf.function as the graph-conversion path for performance-sensitive code (TensorFlow Developers 2024). Its strength is the compiled path from a captured graph to many deployment runtimes.

TensorFlow Developers. 2024. Better Performance with tf.function.

7.8.2 PyTorch

Where TensorFlow’s design centers the path from captured graph to deployment runtime, PyTorch centers developer experience. PyTorch was built from the start on dynamic graphs (or “Define-by-Run”). Instead of building a graph before execution, PyTorch builds the computational graph as the code runs, because researchers need immediate feedback when experimenting with novel architectures and a define-then-run cycle slows that loop. Python loops, conditionals, and debuggers work directly inside a model’s forward pass, with no special syntax and no separate compilation step, which matters most while architectures and training objectives are still changing.

PyTorch’s answer to the differentiation problem is tape-based autograd (section 7.4.3.1): flexible and debuggable, but harder to optimize globally because the tape is rebuilt each iteration. Its answer to the abstraction problem is more pragmatic than comprehensive: strong GPU support through cuBLAS and cuDNN, with deployment paths including torch.export, edge-targeted ExecuTorch, ONNX, and specialized runtimes.

The trade-off is therefore a more fragmented deployment path. Because the graph is dynamic, the framework cannot easily perform global optimizations before execution. A model that works perfectly in development may hit performance walls in production when dispatch overhead dominates small operations. To bridge this research-to-production gap, PyTorch added graph-capture and compilation paths (torch.compile and export workflows) that capture a dynamic model and turn it into an optimized representation for deployment. This evolution shows how an eager framework can move toward the production end of the compilation continuum while preserving the interactive experience that motivated the design.

7.8.3 JAX

PyTorch’s eager execution and TensorFlow’s graph compilation represent two points on a spectrum, yet both share an imperative programming heritage where computation proceeds as a sequence of stateful operations. JAX takes a different user-facing approach, built on functional programming principles and composable program transformations rather than object-level tapes or user-authored graph APIs (Bradbury et al. 2018). Developed at Google, JAX is especially useful for work requiring custom differentiation, advanced optimization research, and large-scale distributed training.

Bradbury, James, Roy Frostig, Peter Hawkins, Matthew James Johnson, Chris Leary, Dougal Maclaurin, George Necula, et al. 2018. JAX: Composable Transformations of Python+NumPy Programs.

Where PyTorch and TensorFlow track state through objects and expose autograd through dynamic tapes or graph-capture paths, JAX asks users to write pure Python functions and treats differentiation, vectorization, and compilation as composable program transformations (listing 21). The jax.grad function returns a new function that computes gradients, which can then be transformed again when the interfaces are compatible: vmap(grad(f)) computes per-example gradients, and jit(vmap(grad(f))) compiles that batched computation. Transformation order is semantically meaningful rather than arbitrary.

This functional approach requires pure functions (no side effects) and immutable data (arrays cannot be modified in place). These constraints are stricter than PyTorch’s mutable object model, and they are what make the transformations safe. Because function outputs depend only on inputs, the compiler can reorder, fuse, and parallelize operations without changing results, and one transformation can be applied to the output of another.

Each transformation does one job. jax.grad returns a gradient function; jax.vmap can vectorize a compatible function over mapped axes; and jax.jit can trace and compile a stable transformed function for a particular argument signature. Unlike an iterative Python loop that issues individual operations sequentially with repeated dispatch overhead, jax.vmap pushes the batch dimension directly into the underlying primitives, allowing compatible per-sample matrix-vector operations to become batched matrix-matrix multiplications (GEMM). The compiled program can contain multiple kernels and library calls. Across devices, JAX shards arrays over a device Mesh and compiles with jax.jit, or maps explicitly with jax.shard_map (the successor to the older jax.pmap); gradient aggregation is expressed with collectives such as lax.psum or lax.pmean. These transformations compose according to their types and semantics, and different orders can compute different quantities.

The same minimalist core delegates neural network abstractions to companion libraries (Flax and Equinox) and optimization to Optax. This separation reflects the functional philosophy: the core provides transformations, while libraries build conventional abstractions on top. The trade-off is that production readiness depends on both the transformation model and the maturity of the surrounding libraries, export paths, and operational tooling for the target environment.

The functional constraints pay off where gradient computation is itself the object of study. Custom differentiation, including higher-order gradients and custom vector-Jacobian product (VJP) and Jacobian-vector product (JVP) rules, composes cleanly because pure functions make differentiation rules predictable, which serves optimization research and scientific computing with AD. Compilation-heavy accelerator workloads gain utilization from XLA when the program fits the functional style. In exchange, JAX demands more upfront investment than PyTorch, with explicit state management, a learning curve for the functional paradigm, and compiled code that is harder to debug than eager execution. It fits teams whose requirements match these strengths rather than serving as a default.

7.8.4 Design trade-offs compared

Comparing TensorFlow, PyTorch, and JAX by asking which framework is fastest in the abstract asks the wrong question, because raw speed changes with model shape, batch size, hardware backend, and compiler configuration. The durable difference among the three designs (graph-first vs. eager-first, stateful vs. functional) is what each one lets the system see and optimize. Table 11 therefore maps TensorFlow, PyTorch, and JAX back to the three framework problems: execution visibility, differentiation model, and hardware abstraction path.

The comparison therefore tells an engineer which constraint to profile first. In PyTorch, check whether eager dispatch or graph breaks dominate. In TensorFlow, check whether the captured graph covers the operators and deployment target. In JAX, check whether shapes and purity let XLA compile the program actually executed. A framework-level comparison or leaderboard can orient a decision, but it cannot replace profiling the specific workload on the target hardware.

The same simple network exposes how each design philosophy shapes the code. Listing 22 implements one neural network, a single linear layer mapping ten inputs to one output, across all three frameworks.

These three implementations solve the same mathematical problem, yet each reveals where its framework keeps state and when its computation graph comes into existence. PyTorch binds state and computation together through class inheritance (nn.Module) and evaluates eagerly, building the graph as Python runs. Standard debuggers and control flow therefore work naturally, at the cost of hiding the full computation from an optimizer unless a graph-capture pass is invoked. TensorFlow and Keras also execute eagerly by default in TensorFlow 2, whereas structured models can be captured with tf.function and exported to supported deployment runtimes whenever operator coverage and target conversion allow. By contrast, JAX treats the model as a pure function25 over immutable arrays with no internal object state. It turns grad, vmap (automatic vectorization), and jit (just-in-time compilation26) into composable transformations on stateless functions, in exchange for explicit parameter passing.

25 Pure function: Returns outputs determined by its inputs without relying on untracked side effects; JAX transformations are designed for pure functions. Ordinary Python effects may run during tracing but are not represented in the compiled computation, and impure code can fail or behave unexpectedly. Runtime printing or external effects require supported mechanisms such as jax.debug.print or callbacks; random draws require explicitly managed keys.

26 Just-in-time (JIT) compilation: Traces a function for an argument signature and lowers it to backend-specific executable code; the first call pays tracing and compilation costs; compatible later calls can reuse the cached executable. New shapes, dtypes, devices, or static arguments may trigger another compilation. Both compilation time and cached-call overhead depend on the program, backend, and hardware.

No framework maximizes all three goals simultaneously, and the ten-line program makes the trade visible in source code rather than buried in compiler internals: where state lives, when the graph exists, and what the compiler is allowed to see. Each design trades what the compiler can see against how quickly a person can iterate. Graph capture and ahead-of-time compilation can reduce runtime overhead and intermediate data movement. Eager evaluation shortens the human iteration loop, outside the iron-law runtime equation, at the cost of compiler visibility until a graph-capture path is used. Functional purity gives XLA more freedom to transform a stable traced program. Each philosophy also shapes code syntax, team workflows, debugging practices, and deployment pipelines, so migration costs grow with project-specific integrations and assumptions.

Eager defaults favor iteration; JAX’s traced functions favor the compiler.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Which framework ecosystem in the section is organized around SavedModel as a central boundary between Python-side development and multiple deployment paths such as server serving, mobile conversion, and browser execution?

    1. PyTorch eager execution as originally presented
    2. TensorFlow ecosystem with SavedModel feeding Serving, Lite conversion, and TensorFlow.js paths
    3. Pure NumPy with custom Python socket servers
    4. Scikit-learn with standard pickle deserialization

  2. What core programming-model commitment distinguishes JAX from both PyTorch and TensorFlow in the chapter’s comparison, enabling transformations like jax.grad, jax.vmap, and jax.jit to compose when their contracts align?

    1. Dynamic class inheritance with mutable object references for all layer parameters
    2. Global state mutation across all forward and backward passes
    3. Pure functions over immutable array-like data, with transformations applied to functions rather than hidden mutable objects
    4. Graph capture via AST string parsing of Python source files

  3. Compare the workflow and deployment trade-offs that led PyTorch to dominate academic research while TensorFlow established strong early dominance in enterprise production serving during the late 2010s.

  4. Explain how JAX’s requirement that functions must be pure and free of side effects enables the XLA compiler to generate highly optimized accelerator kernels through jax.jit.
